市場疑慮拖累美股，短線聚焦 Fed 決策與財報
2026 年 9 月 15 日
一、 9/14 美股下挫、美債殖利率曲線熊市趨平，且油價大漲
1. 股票指數：
⚫ 道瓊工業指數下跌 0.29%，收 52,421.20 點；
⚫ S&P 500 指數下挫 0.48%，收 7,619.98 點；
⚫ 那斯達克綜合指數下跌 0.56%，收 26,186.41 點；
⚫ 費城半導體指數下跌 5.86%，收 11,131.28 點。
2. 當日重要個股表現：
⚫ 在科技類股方面，輝達下跌約 3.4%、美光下跌 5.3%、邁威爾下跌 7.3%；

⚫ 通訊類股方面，Netflix 上漲 3.8%、Take-Two 上漲 3.5%、Fox 上漲 4.6%；
⚫ 軟體類股方面 ，ServiceNow、Adobe、Workday 等軟體股則上漲約 4% 至
7.4%。
3. 美國債市漲跌不一、殖利率曲線走勢熊市趨平。2 年期美國公債殖利率上升 3.24 個基
點至 4.66%，10 年期殖利率上升 2.06 個基點至 4.99%，10 年期殖利率一度升破 5%，
而 30 年期殖利率則下跌約 0.76 個基點至 5.34%。短率上升，反映市場對未來美國聯
準會升息的預期。
4. 油價因中東衝突與供給風險再度升高而上漲 ，WTI 原油上漲 1.81%，收在每桶 101.86
美元，布蘭特原油則上漲 1.56%，收在 106.24 美元。
二、 下跌原因分析
1. AI 開發放緩疑慮，壓抑硬體需求預期：Anthropic 執行長 Dario Amodei 於上週末
呼籲放慢前沿 AI 能力提升速度，並提出第三方安全評估與業界協調方案，使 AI 擴

確性不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決
策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
張節奏成為新的不確定因素。我們研判，半導體位於 AI 建置支出的上游，股價對新
增算力投資的成長速度尤其敏感。即使既有訂單未立即改變，只要市場降低對未來數
季增速的期待，GPU、高頻寬記憶體及網通設備供應鏈的評價便可能先行調整；目前
仍須區分投資預期降溫與實際訂單下修。
2. 中東能源設施遇襲，油價推升通膨風險：沙烏地阿拉伯能源設施及中東船舶遇襲，引
發供應疑慮，布蘭特原油期貨 9 月 14 日結算價升至每桶 106.24 美元。油價持續偏
高可能透過運輸與生產成本以及民眾實質購買力影響企業獲利；若進一步傳導至通膨

預期，將延長貨幣政策維持緊縮的時間，對股市評價形成壓力。
3. 十年美債殖利率一度突破 5%，評價與融資承壓：美國 10 年期公債殖利率盤中突破
5%，為 2023 年以來首見。殖利率上升提高未來獲利的折現率，對依賴長期成長預
期支撐評價的股票較不利。另一方面，資料中心若仰賴借款擴張，融資成本上升也會
提高投資報酬門檻，可能影響新增建置計畫，形成 AI 供應鏈面臨的另一項壓力。
4. 通膨月增回升 ， 升息前觀望氣氛加重 ：BLS 於 9 月 11 日公布 ，8 月 CPI 月增 0.4%
，高於 7 月的 0.1%，核心 CPI 月增率也由 0.2% 升至 0.3%。我們認為，短期物
價動能回升，加上能源價格偏高，提高聯準會維持緊縮政策的壓力。投資人除了評估
當次升息，也須重新衡量後續利率高點與維持時間，增加 FOMC 會議前高波動股票
的調整壓力。
5. 成長與評價雙重重估，放大半導體跌幅：本波股市回檔壓力同時來自獲利成長預期與
市場願給的評價倍數。AI 開發放緩疑慮影響未來營收想像，殖利率上行則壓低未來獲

利的現值。當股價已反映較高成長期待，只要新增訊息不如預期，即使當期營收仍強
勁，也可能出現明顯修正。後續應觀察獲利上修能否抵銷評價收縮，不能僅以跌幅大
就認定股價便宜。

不另行通知。
三、市場看法
1. 龍頭財報仍具成長，支持中期正向看法：輝達 8 月 26 日公布的 2027 會計年度第
二季資料中心營收達 890 億美元、年增 117%，並預估第三季總營收 1,080 億美元
、上下浮動 2%。博通 9 月 2 日公布的 2026 會計年度第三季 AI 半導體營收為
167 億美元、年增 221%，並預估第四季達 217 億美元。上述實績與公司展望，是
我們對 AI 核心供應鏈維持中期正向看法的依據；惟這些財測發布於本次事件之前，
仍須追蹤後續更新。
2. 放緩方案尚待落地，需求影響須分項評估：Anthropic 執行長 Amodei 明確表示，放

緩不代表停止模型訓練，但也提出研議訓練算力等限制，因此實際影響取決於後續執
行方式。既有模型的商業使用、推論與安全測試仍可能帶來算力需求，惟能否抵銷前
沿訓練投資的放緩，必須由使用量、訂單與收入驗證。此時適合保留成長觀點，同時
提高對支出變化的敏感度。
3. 科技股仍有輪動，軟體與半導體強弱分明：9/14 當日企業軟體與資安股逆勢上漲，顯
示科技股內部仍有輪動與受惠程度的差異。我們認為，若前沿 AI 開發放緩，部分既
有軟體業者面臨的競爭及產品替代壓力可能減輕；AI 安全疑慮升高，也可能強化資安
、權限控管及企業治理需求。相較之下，半導體更直接承受新增建置支出預期變化。
因此，9/14 當日費半重挫尚不足以推論整體科技需求同步轉弱；後續宜比較訂單能見
度、訂閱收入、獲利與現金流，單日相對強勢仍須由基本面延續性驗證。
4. 升息機率約九成，會後政策態度才是關鍵：依 Reuters 於美東 9 月 14 日收盤報導
引述的 CME FedWatch 資料，本次升息一碼的機率約 90%。若美東 9 月 16 日

FOMC 確實升息 0.25 個百分點，利率區間將升至 3.75% 至 4.00%，當次升息已
大致反映於市場預期。我們認為，會後聲明及主席記者會對未來利率政策的態度才是
關鍵：是否暗示還需升息、利率須維持高檔多久，以及是否擔心能源漲價向核心通膨

確性不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決
策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
擴散，並須搭配點陣圖判讀。若後續緊縮訊號未超出預期、長債殖利率趨穩，有利評
價修復；若利率路徑較預期更高、更持久，科技股仍可能承壓。
5. 通膨改善仍不均衡，避免過早判定轉折：8 月核心 CPI 年增率為 2.4%，低於 7 月
的 2.5%，但其月增率由 0.2% 升至 0.3%，顯示年增放緩與短期動能回升並存。後
續須觀察能源漲價是否向服務價格與通膨預期擴散。若油價回穩、核心物價再度降溫
，利率壓力可望緩和；若能源衝擊持續，股市調整時間可能拉長。
四、投資建議
1. 短線聚焦 Fed 決策與 10 月美股財報，科技股中期向上趨勢料不致有變：我們目前仍

維持 AI 核心供應鏈中期正向看法，較看好能將營收成長轉化為獲利與現金流的企業
，但宜密切觀察本週的 FOMC 會議結果與 10 月中將起跑的美股財報。若雲端業者維
持資本支出、晶片公司財測穩定，且 FOMC 此次會議未釋出超預期緊縮訊號，有利
市場信心修復；然而，若出現廣泛支出下修、訂單遞延，或油價與殖利率持續上行，
則應拉長觀察期並重新檢視通膨與利率對於股市動能前景的影響。
2. 3~10 年美國公債/投資級債為債市首選：目前美國的通膨數據仍具韌性，且就業市場
穩健，使年底前市場升息預期再度升溫。市場對美國財政赤字、公債供給及長期通膨
仍要求較高風險補償，30 年期殖利率與短天期利差仍維持高檔，顯示期限溢酬尚未收
斂，考量當前 3～10 年期美國公債與投資級債能鎖定目前較高息收，且其受長端財政
風險影響也相對較小，故現階段3～10 年期美國公債及投資級債的風險報酬結構相對
較佳。

市場焦點
美國
• Fed褐皮書顯示，美國經濟整體維持溫和擴張，10個地區經濟活動小幅至溫和成長，消費及企
業需求仍有支撐 ，但住宅市場偏弱 、消費者價格敏感度升高 。就業僅小幅增加 ，薪資溫和成
長；另一方面 ，能源、運輸、原物料及關稅成本持續推升企業支出 ，物價在多數地區溫和上
升，反映經濟韌性與通膨壓力並存，增加Fed政策判斷難度。
美伊衝突
• 美國８月底宣布對逾 60個伊朗相關實體實施制裁 ，試圖切斷伊朗的石油 、金融與貿易收入 。
但伊朗歷經數十年制裁後 ，已建立相對成熟的制裁規避網絡 ，但短期預期美國仍難迫使德黑
蘭屈服。對金融市場而言 ，若制裁未進一步衝擊伊朗石油出口 ，油價上行空間相對有限；反
之，若美國擴大次級制裁並波及中國等主要買家，則可能推升原油價格與全球通膨預期。

韓股
• 儘管市場波動劇烈 ，韓國企業在第⼆季度財報季的盈利前景仍在持續改善 ，在三星電⼦和 SK
海力⼠公佈業績後，MSCI韓國指數的全年每股純益預期被小幅上調 ，此前5至6月修正步伐一
度放緩，如今⼜重新加快，整體來看，今年韓國盈利預期累計上調幅度接近 190%，並推動整
個新興市場盈利預期提升約 37%，晶⽚需求指引依然強勁 ，隨著盈利預測持續上調 ，台灣市
場也從中受益。

美國製造業維持擴張，需求降溫與高成本壓力牽制擴張動能
• 新訂單降溫，製造業維持擴張韌性 ：美國8月ISM製造業PMI降至54.6%，較7月55.6%回落，但仍連續第8個月位於擴張
區間；新訂單降至 53.7%、訂單積壓降至 51.8%，顯示需求動能較前月放緩 ，惟生產指數仍達 58.3%，連續第10個月擴
張，運輸設備、機械及石油與煤炭等主要產業維持成長，製造業整體仍具韌性。
• 生產韌性延續，價格壓力仍偏高：就業指數降至51.2%，雖較前月走弱但仍處擴張區間；供應商交貨指數升至59.3%，代
表供應商交期進一步拉長 ，而價格指數維持71.1%，原物料價格已連續 23個月上升，鋼鋁、關稅及石化產品成本仍是主
要壓力來源。整體呈現「需求降溫、生產偏強、成本仍高」的格局，企業利潤與通膨傳導仍需觀察。

• 後市觀察：製造業景氣仍具韌性 ，但新訂單放緩與投入成本高檔並存 ，後續應觀察需求能否延續 ，以及鋼鋁、能源與關
稅成本是否進一步轉嫁至終端價格上 。投資布局可聚焦半導體 、電機電⼦及AI基礎建設等受惠於製造投資與 AI資本支出
的領域；債券則以中短天期及投資等級公司債為主，降低利率波動風險，以股債配置分散景氣與利率風險。

褐皮書揭美國經濟溫和成長，成本壓力增添Fed政策難度
• 經濟溫和擴張，消費與企業需求仍有支撐：Fed褐皮書
顯示，自7月初至8月下旬，10個地區經濟活動小幅至
溫和成長、2個地區持平，消費支出整體小幅增加 ，製
造業、部分服務業及金融活動有所改善 ，資料中心與
國防相關需求亦提供支撐；但住宅市場表現仍偏弱 ，
消費者對價格更加敏感 ，顯示經濟維持韌性但內需表
現分化。
• 就業增幅有限 ，成本壓力仍牽動通膨 ：就業整體僅小
幅增加，7個地區就業成長 、5個地區大致持平 ，薪資
多數維持溫和上升；然而能源 、運輸、金屬、石化原
料及關稅成本仍持續推升企業投入成本 ，部分企業甚
至難以完全轉嫁成本，整體價格仍呈現上升。
• 市場觀察： 褐皮書反映美國經濟尚未明顯轉弱 ，但就

業降溫與通膨壓力並存，使Fed政策取向更具挑戰。近
期市場對9月升息的預期明顯升溫 ，政策仍高度依賴後
續通膨與就業數據 。建議債券配置宜以短中天期美債
及投資級公司債為主 ，降低長天期利率波動；股票則
聚焦大型優質科技 、AI基礎建設及高現金流企業 ，並
搭配黃金分散通膨與地緣政治風險。

制裁使伊朗經濟受創，仍難迫使政權屈服
上海合作組織(SCO)佔全球經濟(GDP)的四分之一
資料來源：IMF，Bloomberg，2026.9.1，其他上海合作組織成員國包括巴基斯坦、伊朗、哈薩克、烏茲別克、
白俄羅斯、蒙古、吉爾吉斯、塔吉克和阿富汗資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.8.26
伊朗絕大部分石油銷往中國
• 制裁聲勢強，但關鍵仍在中國：美國８月底宣布對逾60個伊朗相關實體實施制裁，試圖切斷伊朗的石油、金融與貿易收入。
制裁能否真正奏效，關鍵仍在於是否將中國納入主要打擊對象。伊朗近八成的石油銷往中國，美方現階段僅將少數中港企業
列入黑名單，刻意避開了中國大型國有金融與能源巨頭，若強行實施全面制裁，可能危及關鍵礦產供應、破壞脆弱的美中貿
易休兵，甚至推升全球能源價格。

• 制裁使伊朗經濟受創，仍難迫使政權屈服：伊朗歷經數十年制裁後，已建立相對成熟的制裁規避網絡，並透過中國、俄羅斯、
印度及中亞國家參與的上海合作組織（SCO）等非西方平台維持經貿往來。俄羅斯與伊朗合作持續深化，巴基斯坦亦表態無
義務遵守美方制裁，印度則在制裁壓力下維持一定程度的經貿交流。因此，美國新一輪制裁雖將加劇伊朗經濟壓力，但短期
仍難迫使德黑蘭屈服。對金融市場而言，若制裁未進一步衝擊伊朗石油出口，油價上行空間相對有限；反之，若美國擴大次
級制裁並波及中國等主要買家，則可能推升原油價格與全球通膨預期，進而延後聯準會降息時程，增加股債市場波動。

歐元區通膨率升至近三年新高，９月升息機率高
• 歐洲央行9月升息機率高：歐洲央行（ECB）9月升息至2.50%的預期已逐步升溫，主因歐元區8月通膨升至3.3%，加上能源
價格受伊朗戰事推升，強化鷹派對進一步緊縮的主張。不過，核心通膨降至2.4%、服務通膨趨緩，且長期通膨預期仍大致穩
定，顯示能源價格尚未明顯向薪資與核心物價擴散，因此，9月升息後暫停仍是較可能的政策路徑。 9月升息後，ECB是否於
12月再次升息，將取決於能源衝擊的持續時間及其向核心通膨的傳導程度。
• 12月是否再升息，能源價格是關鍵：若伊朗戰事延續、油氣價格維持高檔，鷹派可能推動利率於12月進一步升至2.75%；反
之，若能源價格回落、服務通膨持續降溫，則本輪升息可能在9月告終。整體而言，ECB政策已轉向偏鷹，但在缺乏能源衝

擊廣泛傳導至核心通膨的明確證據下，年內連續升息空間有限。在政策利率逐步觸頂的情況下，歐債殖利率若因升息預期而
上行，反而可提供較佳的中長期配置與收益機會。
歐元區通膨（CPI） ECBspeak指數暗示歐洲央行還會進一步加息

SK海力⼠公佈業績後，MSCI韓國指數的全年每股純益預期被小幅上調 ，此前5至6月修正步伐一度放緩，如今⼜重新加快，整

望成為第三季韓股上漲的重要動能，尤其是記憶體產業龍頭企業，擁有充沛的自由現金流，實施庫藏股買回，不僅能提升每股
價值，也反映管理層對未來獲利前景具信心，並認為目前股價被低估，參考日本推動公司治理改革後帶動股市重評價的經驗 ，

預期韓股後市仍具支撐力，若市場出現短期回檔，反而可視為逢低布局的機會。

美債市場結構性轉變：從不挑剔的機構到追求回報的私人投資者
• 自新冠疫情以來 ，美國10年期公債殖利率持續走高 ，過去市場多半歸咎於聯準會升息或通膨 ，但彭博最新研究顯示 ，期限
溢價的顯著上升才是主要推手，其背後的根本原因是美債買家發生了結構性轉變。
• 傳統非價格敏感型買家縮手：過去幾十年，聯準會(透過QE)與外國央行是美債的兩大底氣來源，他們買美債是為了儲備與流
動性，對價格不敏感，因此能有效壓低利率與期限溢價 。自2020年以來，聯準會與外國官方持有的美債佔美國 GDP比重分
別下降12%與8%，光是這兩大買家的退場，就推升10年期美債期限溢價約1.05%。
• 私人投資者接棒，市場對殖利率極度挑剔： 目前的邊際買家已轉為外國私人投資者 (如基金、保險公司等)，私人買家會精算

美債、公司債、股票與各國主權債的收益差異，進行嚴格比價。當美債發行量增加時，必須給予更高的殖利率才能吸引他們
購買，這使得美債利率波動更加劇烈。
自疫情以來，長期利率呈上升趨勢

供給端新挑戰：AI浪潮帶動公司債暴增，搶奪資金池
• 科技巨頭發債融資潮 ，公司債成為美債的強勁替代品： 受人工智慧建設資本支出帶動 ，大型科技企業 (如Alphabet、Meta、
Amazon等)積極發行投資級公司債 。這些高品質科技公司債提供優異回報 ，吸引了大量私人資金從美債轉向公司債 。為了
與這些高收益的高評級公司債競爭資金，美國國債殖利率面臨更大的上行壓力。
• 小結及建議：當前美債殖利率居高不下，核心原因在於穩定的買家減少，而接手的私人買家變得挑剔，加上AI熱潮創造了高
回報的公司債競爭源。在美國財政部發債壓力未減的情況下，長端殖利率短期內難大幅下行。債券策略建議採取配置中短天
期債券，長天期公債(10年期以上)價格受期限溢價與供給壓力壓制，波動較大，建議縮短持債年期(如布局3-5年期)，鎖定高

殖利率的同時降低價格波動風險 ，加碼高品質投資級公司債，目前私人資金偏好高評級公司債(尤其是科技與AI龍頭債)，這
類債券信用風險極低，收益率卻優於同天期公債，是當前固定收益資產的優質選擇。
外國私人投資者購買美國公債和公司債
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.1
人工智慧相關債務發行只是殖利率上升的原因之一

日圓再現匯率干預跡象 套利交易平倉恐加劇日圓升值
• 日圓再現匯率干預跡象：上週週間，日圓兌美元匯價出現單日大幅升值情況，市場再度懷疑官方出手干預匯率。然而，彭博
指出，日本央行帳戶數據沒有大規模買入日元跡象，意味著匯率的劇烈波動可能來自市場重新調整日本央行加息預期，而非
日本政府再次入市干預。與此同時，美國財政部並未回應是否參與干預。
• 市場懷疑干預之際，套利交易平倉可能正在發生：儘管無法證實官方上週是否直接干預日圓匯價，但日圓走升可能也反映上
週日本央行官員高田創釋出鷹派升息的態度，將不排除一次性大幅升息或連續升息的可能性。根據芝加哥商品交易所的數據
顯示，日圓融資的套利交易正在平倉，而且中期內持有日圓而非其他G10貨幣的意願非常強烈。
• 觀點：2024年8月日本央行意外升息，引發日圓套利交易平倉，全球主要國家股市短期間出現修正，當時AI與半導體成為套

利資金撤離重點。本次匯價波動暫時可歸咎於套利交易倉位的調整，尚未出現恐慌賣壓，重點將關注在本月中的日本央行利
率會議，留意意外大幅升息的風險。
資料來源：Bloomberg，截至2026.9.3。

• 今年年初黃金價格創下歷史新高，突破每盎司5,000美元大關，成為金融市場最受關注的資產之一，隨後經歷幾個月的高檔
修正後，上個月再度獲得市場資金追捧，現貨黃金價格單月大漲近10%。過去市場認為黃金上漲主要來自地緣政治風險與央
行降息預期，但近期投資邏輯似乎正在改變。
• 黃金多頭始自央行「去美元化」：本輪黃金多頭始自2022年烏俄衝突，西方國家凍結俄羅斯央行的大部分海外美元與歐元
外匯儲備，讓各國央行開始意識到外匯儲備的安全性，進而推動外匯儲備多元化。透過適度減持美元(美債)來佈局實體黃金
或其他國家貨幣，被成為「去美元化」趨勢。
• 「貶值交易」有望接棒黃金上漲動能：上個月美國公布最新國債規模已突破40兆美元，市場開始擔憂財政赤字的可維持性，
貨幣信心下降導致「貶值交易」發生(美債信心下降)，除了導致長天期美債殖利率上揚之外，貨幣避險需求再度讓黃金資產

成為市場焦點，8月份現貨黃金一度上漲超過15%，隨後雖因聯準會釋出鷹派訊號，單月漲幅仍接近10%。

• 央行買盤仍為黃金中期表現帶來利多：根據世界黃金協會2026年各國央行調查，上半年截至5/19，有45%的央行預期未來
一年將增加自身黃金儲備，創調查9年來新高。央行增持黃金主要考量包含：儲備多元化、美元走弱預期、地緣政治衝突以
及金價長期上漲趨勢，顯示黃金已成為各國儲備資產的重要戰略配置，在強勁的需求買盤下，多數機構預測黃金將維持多頭
至明年年底。
• 觀點：近期黃金價格反彈後，可能會受到獲利了結以及利率政策預期變化而出現波動，但在全球債務問題、貨幣貶值風險及
央行買盤支撐下，在中期內仍有機會走出慢牛行情。建議投資人以資產配置的角度，逢修正適度配置黃金資產(如：黃金ETF)。
風險接受度較高的投資人，則可關注金礦類股，但需注意其對於美元匯、利率的敏感度較高，資產價格波動較大。

2. 本資料提及之經濟走勢預測不必然代表基金之績效，基金投資風險請詳閱基金公開說明書。
3. 基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良管理人之注意義務外，不負責本基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益 ，投資人申購
前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
4. 本資料所載內容如涉及商品資訊 ，商品相關資訊仍以經核可之條款及文宣 (DM)為主，本行網站已備有公開說明書可供查閱基金應負擔之費用及相關投資風險 。投
資人申購基金係持有基金受益憑證，而非本資料提及之投資資產或標的。
5. 本資料所載內容取自本公司相信可靠之資料來源，且為特定日期之判斷，本公司不保證其即時性、精確性及完整性，亦不提供或嘗試遊說客戶做為投資之判斷依據。

資料內容邇後若有變更，本公司將不做預告或主動更新。投資人於決策時應審慎衡量本身風險，如因信賴本資料內容而做出或改變投資決策，須自行承擔結果，本
公司及本公司所屬員工，均不負任何損害賠償及其他法律上之責任。
6. 本資料內容之著作權屬兆豐銀行所有，禁止就所載資料任何形式之抄襲、引用或轉載。
7. 本資料所載部分基金涉及非投資等級債，投資人投資以非投資等級債券為訴求之基金不宜占其投資組合過高之比重 。基金經金管會核准，惟不表示絕無風險。由於
非投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，故本基金可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支
付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損。部分基金不適合無法承擔相關風險之投資人。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良
管理人之注意義務外不負責本基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
8. 基金年化配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除息日前一日之淨值×一年配息次數×100%。「年化配息率」為估算值。當月配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除

息日前一日之淨值×100%。
9. 基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動。投資人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績
效亦不代表未來績效之保證。本資料如以基金過去績效進行模擬投資組合之報酬率時，僅為歷史資料模擬投資組合之結果，不代表本投資組合之實際報酬率及未來
績效保證，不同時間進行模擬操作，其結果亦可能不同。
10. 部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之相關費用。基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部份，可能導致原始投資金額減損。各基金由本
金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至各投信基金公司及總代理人網站查詢。

Hon Hai (2317.TW): Sep beat: revenues +38% YoY; New AI servers, CPO 
switch and Foldable phones to drive 4Q26; Buy (on CL)
5 October 2026 | 11:23PM HKT
Hon Hai’s Sep revenues were +38% YoY/ 26% MoM to NT$1.2tn, or 15% / 21% 
higher than our / Bloomberg consensus estimates, reflecting strong AI servers ramp 
up and new smartphone models in contribution. From a MoM perspective, 
Consumer electronics is the strongest, followed by Components, Cloud and 
Networking and Computing, and all segments are positive MoM. The strong MoM 
reaffirms our positive view on the company’s leading market position across: (1) 
Smartphone: serving global leading brands, which suffer less under high memory 
costs, with new model launches and form factor changes (report link), driving the 
shipment and ASP increase, (2) AI servers: serving global leading CSP customers, 
and early in the delivery of the latest AI server racks, driving the shipment and ASP 
increase. The company is also the major supplier of CPO switch, supporting Sep and 
4Q26 growth. From a YoY perspective, Cloud and Networking is the strongest, 
followed by Components, Computing, and Consumer electronics, and all segments 
are positive YoY. We attribute the slower YoY in consumer electronics to its major 
brand customers’ fewer model launches this year, along with the new form factor 
(foldable phones) being not available in Sep, and a modest smartphone market 
under high memory costs.

3-month revenues preview: We expect company’s 4Q26E revenues to be +37% 
YoY/ +18% QoQ to NT$3.6 trillion. 3Q26 delivered strong revenues YoY growth of 
47%, or 5% / 7% ahead of our / Bloomberg consensus, and we expect to see both 
YoY and MoM growth throughout Oct-Dec, driven by new AI server racks, new 
smartphone models, and CPO switches. Management notes that AI-related products

EV business in Sep 2026: Hon Hai’s EV subsidiary Foxtron (2258.TW, not covered)

server racks ramp up, Hon Hai’s leading market position in terms of scale, technology, 
and execution, and rising foldable phones to drive growth ahead. We keep 2027E/ 
2028E estimates largely unchanged.

Valuation: Our 12m TP for Hon Hai is unchanged at NT$400 and remains based on a 
P/E. Our target 2027E P/E multiple of 17.9x (unchanged) is derived from a peers’ 
average ratio of trading P/E to forward year NI YoY growth and OPM (P/E divided by the 
sum of NI YoY and OPM). Our target P/E of 17.9x is at the high-end of the company’s 
historical forward P/E, reflecting our positive view on Hon Hai’s business expansion from 
the competitive consumer electronics market to AI servers and EVs outsourcing, which 
should drive profit expansion with diversifying business opportunities for Hon Hai. 
Maintain Buy (on CL).

Price Target Risks and Methodology – Hon Hai 
Valuation methodology: We are Buy rated on Hon Hai. Our 12-month target price of 
NT$400 is based on an 17.9x 2027E P/E multiple, which is set in line with peers’ PEG&M 
ratio (i.e., P/E vs. forward-year earnings growth and OPM). 
Key downside risks: (1) slower-than-expected ramp-up of the AI server business; (2) 
weaker-than-expected EV total solution performance across EV assembly, design,

Ratings, coverage universe and related definitions
Buy (B), Neutral (N), Sell (S) Analysts recommend stocks as Buys or Sells for inclusion on various regional Investment Lists. Being assigned a Buy or Sell
on an Investment List is determined by a stock’s total return potential relative to its coverage universe. Any stock not assigned as a Buy or a Sell on an
Investment List with an active rating (i.e., a  stock that is not Rating Suspended, Not Rated, Early-Stage Biotech, Coverage Suspended or Not Covered), is

deemed Neutral. Each region manages Regional Conviction Lists, which are selected from Buy rated stocks on the respective region’s Investment Lists
and represent investment recommendations focused on the size of the total return potential and/or the likelihood of the realization of the return across
their respective areas of coverage.  The addition or removal of stocks from such Conviction Lists are managed by the Investment Review Committee or
other designated committee in each respective region and do not represent a change in the analysts’ investment rating for such stocks.

Total return potential represents the upside or downside differential between the current share price and the price target, including all paid or
anticipated dividends, expected during the time horizon associated with the price target. Price targets are required for all covered stocks. The total
return potential, price target and associated time horizon are stated in each report adding or reiterating an Investment List membership.
Coverage Universe: A list of all stocks in each coverage universe is available by primary analyst, stock and coverage universe at

https://www.gs.com/research/hedge.html.

주가 추이

3Q 큰 폭의 판매 감소, 매출/영업이익 시장 기대치 하회 예상 
3Q26 동사 매출액과 영 업 이 익 은 각각 44.6조원(-4.5% 이하 Y oY)과 2.4조원
(-5.4%) 기록하며 시장 기대치 하회 전망. 3Q 매출액은 글로벌 판매 부진 지속
과 환율 하락, 그리고 친환경차 판매 성장 둔화 등의 영향에 기인. 특히, 3Q 판
매량은 918.7만대(-11.5% 이하 Y oY)로 전년 동기 대비 큰 폭 감소하며 매출
액에 부정적 영향. 이는 7월과 8월 발생한 파업 영향으로 약 5.5만대 규모의 생
산 물량 차질이 발 생 한 부분에 기인. 파업은 기존 물량/믹스 개선에 기 여 했 던 
글로벌 HEV 판매도 둔화(+3.4%)시키면서 매출에 부정적 영향을 심화. 
3Q 수 익 성 은 4~5월 상승했던 원자재 투입에 따른 원가 상승 영향과 친환경차 
판매 감소에 따른 부정적 믹스 영향 불 가 피. 이에 시장 기대치인 3.0조원을 크
게 하회할 전망. 다만, 기말 환율이 1,356원/$ 수준으로 낮아지면서 판매보증비 
외화환산손익 영향은 다소 개선(0.4%p) 예상되며, 관세 영향 또한 전년 동기 
대비 약 1조원(2.1%p) 감소하면서 수익성 악화 영향을 일부 만 회 할 전망.  
’27년 지연된 신차효과 가시화로 반등 여력 충분 
4Q부터는 3Q 파업 영향 등으로 지연된 신차 확대 본격화되고, 현지 수요 높은 
차종 중심의 물량 확대를 통해 매출 회복 및 이익 성장 가시화가 기대. 아 반 떼
와 투싼 FMC 확대로 국내 및 해외 판매 둔화가 점진적 만회되고, 지난 9월부
터 터키에서 아이오닉3 생산이 본격화되면서 유럽에서의 시장 부진 회복이 기
대. 또한, GV80과 G80 HEV 모델 출시로 내년부터 국내/북미 중심 판매 확대
되며 올해 부진했던 물량/믹스 개선에 기여할 것으로 기대. ’27년 HMGMA에
서 HEV 신차 생산 투입 예정되어 있어 상 대 적 으 로 높은 수요가 지속되는 북미
에서의 HEV 현지 생산 확대로 가동률 증 가 와 관세 부담 완화도 수익성 개선에 
기여할 것으로 기대. 
부정적 환율 고려 필요하나 ’27년 강한 증익 기대  
’27년 동사 영 업 이 익 추 정 치 는 12.4조원(+22.3%)으로 ’26년 감 익 에 서 ’27년 
증익 전환 예상. 최근 환율 추세 고려 시 내년 상 반 기 까 지 부정적인 환율 영향 
확대 가 능 성 존재하나, 기존 노후 차 종 에 서 현지 수요 대응 가능한 신차 중심 
생산/판매 확대로 물량/믹스 개선되며 부 정 적 환율 영향 상쇄 기대.  
12M EPS 추정치 하향(-11.8%)과 상반기 적 용 한 코 스 피 프리미엄 제거(-
10%)에 따라 목 표 주 가 를 670,000원으로 하향. 그러나, 연 내 RMAC 운영 
성 과 와 RA 설립, 12 월 모셔널 자 율 주 행 상용화와 웨 이 모向 로 보 택 시 
양산/공급 등 미래 사 업 에 서 의 가 시 성 도 여전히 기대 가능한 부분.

[ Compliance Notice ] (공표일: 2026 년 10월 8 일) 
이 자료는 조사분석 담당자가 객관적 사실에 근거해 작성하였으며, 타인의 부당한 압력이나 간섭없이 본인의 견을 정확하게 
반영했습니다. 본인은 이 자료에서 다룬 종목과 관련해 공표일 현재 관련 법규상 알려야 할 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다. 본인은 이 
자료를 기관투자자 또는 제 3 자에게 사전에 제공한 사실이 없습니다. (김성래) 
저희 회사는 공표일 현재 이 자료에서 다룬 종목의 발행주식을 1% 이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다. 
이 자료는 투자자의 증권투자를 돕기 위해 당사 고객에 한하여 배포되는 자료로서 저작권이 당사에 있으며 불법 복제 및 배포를 금
합니다. 이 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사 리서치센터가 신뢰할 만한 자료나 정보출처로부터 얻은 것이지만, 당사는 그 정확성이나 완
전성을 보장할 수 없습니다. 따라서 이 자료는 어떠한 경우에도 고객의 증권투자 결과와 관련된 법적 책임소재에 대한 증빙으로 사
용될 수 없습니다.

### Page 8

현대차 (005380) [한화리서치]

8 
[ 종목 투자등급 ] 
당사는 개별 종목에 대해 향후 1 년간 +15% 이상의 절대수익률이 기대되는 종목에 대해 Buy(매수) 의견을 제시합니다. 또한 절대수
익률 -15~+15%가 예상되는 종목에 대해 Hold(보유) 의견을, -15% 이하가 예상되는 종목에 대해 Sell(매도) 의견을 제시합니다. 밸류
에이션 방법 등 절대수익률 산정은 개별 종목을 커버하는 애널리스트의 추정에 따르며, 목표주가 산정이나 투자의견 변경 주기는 종
목별로 다릅니다.

[ 산업 투자의견 ] 
당사는 산업에 대해 향후 1 년간 해당 업종의 수익률이 과거 수익률에 비해 양호한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Positive(긍정
적) 의견을 제시하고 있습니다. 또한 향후 1 년간 수익률이 과거 수익률과 유사한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Neutral(중립적) 
의견을, 과거 수익률보다 부진한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Negative(부정적) 의견을 제시하고 있습니다. 산업별 수익률 전
망은 해당 산업 내 분석대상 종목들에 대한 담당 애널리스트의 분석과 판단에 따릅니다.

[ 당사 조사분석자료의 투자등급 부여 비중 ] (기준일: 2026 년 9 월 30 일)

3Q26Pre: 환율 하락에도 성장 흐름 유지 
매출액 1조 3,477억원(+7.2%YoY, 이하 YoY 생략), 영업이익 5,957억원(-6.0%, OPM 
44.2%)로 컨센서스에 부합할 것으로 전망. 3분기 원달러 평균 환율은 1,423원으로 전분기 
평균 환율 대비 약 5.2% 하락에도 불구하고 성장 흐름 지속. 
2분기 노조 파업으로 인한 영향은 3분기 생산을 진행했으며 4분기 매출에 반영될 예정으로 
연간 실적에 미치는 영향은 제한적일 것으로 예상. GSK로부터 인수한 록빌 공장도 안정적
으로 생산이 이루어지고 있으며 CAPA 여유분은 추가 수주를 진행 중으로 28~29년 이후 
완전 가동 전망. 
4Q, 6공장 건설과 폴리펩타이드 인수 
유상 증자 관련 투자 설명서 상 투자 계획에 따르면 6공장 투자는 26년부터 29년까지 진행 
될 예정으로 29년 가동 목표. 6공장은 5공장과 동일한 18만 리터 규모로 건설되며, 연내 투
자 집행을 시작함에 따라 건설 발표도 임박한 것으로 추정. 동사는 일정 수준 이상 기존 공
장 수주를 확보한 후 후속 공장 건설을 진행해오고 있어 non-binding 수주 계약의 본계약 
전환도 순차적으로 이루어질 것으로 전망. 
폴리펩타이드 인수도 현재 공개 매수를 진행 중에 있으며 연내 마무리될 것으로 예상. 
1H26 폴리펩타이드 매출은 2.37억 유로(+41.6%)로 GLP-1 agonist 등 펩타이드 의약품 
수요 증가에 따라 높은 성장 지속. 
투자 의견 Buy, 목표 주가 200만원으로 하향 
매수 의견을 유지하며 목표 주가는 기존 210만원에서 200만원으로 하향. 목표 주가 하향은 
유상 증자에 따른 EPS 희석에 기인. 글로벌 항체 CDMO 수요가 여전히 견조하며, 폴리펩
타이드 인수로 펩타이드로의 확장에 따른 추가적인 성장 기대. 주주 대상 유상 증자 영향과 
파업 우려 등이 있으나 노사 합의 시 주가 반등 전망.

삼성바이오로직스 (207940)

Appendix

Document 131: [329180] HD현대중공업(329180) HD현대중공업 증설 공시 코멘트HD현대중공업(329180) HD현대중공업 증설 공시 코멘트HD현대중공업 증설 공시 코멘트 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[329180] HD현대중공업(329180) HD현대중공업 증설 공시 코멘트HD현대중공업(329180) HD현대중공업 증설 공시 코멘트HD현대중공업 증설 공시 코멘트 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-09-11
Pages: 4
Status: ok

### Page 1

HD현대중공업은 전일(9/10) 대출력 발전엔진 (20MW, 9MW 급) 및 SMR 캐파 
확장을 위한 신규시설투자를 공시했다. 규모는 총 1조 722억원이며, 그 중 엔진 관련 
투자는 8,336억원, SMR 관련 투자는 2,386억원이다.

엔진 관련 투자에서는 4행정 중속엔진 캐파 확장을 위한 신규 공장 신설 및 기존 
공장 생산 설비 구축을 진행하고자 한다. 동사의 4행정 중속엔진 캐파는 기존 3GW 
규모이며, 이번 증설을 통해 신규 공장 캐파 3GW, 기존 공장 증설분 1.2GW가 
더해져 2028년 5월 기준 최종적으로 7.2GW 규모가 될 것으로 예상된다. 영암 
공장과 신축 공장에서는 데이터센터향으로 납품될 9MW 및 20MW 규모의 대출력 
엔진 위주, 기존 울산 공장은 3~5MW 규모의 선박용 엔진 위주의 생산이 이루어질 
계획이다. 이번 공장 신축 완료 이후에도 가용 부지가 남아있어 시장 수요에 따라 
향후 추가적인 캐파 확대 역시 고려할 수 있다.

SMR 투자의 경우 현대건설/테라파워와의 협력을 위한 SMR 주기기 연 2기 생산 
캐파 확보를 목적으로 진행되었다. 1기 당 출력 용량은 345MW이며, 구체적인 생산 
단가 및 물량의 경우 테라파워사와 지속 협의 중으로 동사의 미래 먹거리로서 
중장기적인 성장성에 기여할 전망이다.

데이터센터향 수요 성장에 대한 확신은 실제 엔진 업체들의 수주 인콰이어리 및 신규 
투자에서 드러나고 있다. 엔진 발전 시장 상위 업체 바르질라와 에버런스가 올해 
결정한 증설 규모는 총 4.5GW로 파악되며, 동사 역시 같은 행보로 신규 시장에서 
유의미한 점유율을 확보할 것으로 기대된다. 동사의 데이터센터향 4행정 중속엔진 
수주 단가를 고려했을 때 GW당 약 1조원의 매출 창출이 가능하다. 또한, 현재 동사 
엔진사업부의 캐시카우를 담당하고 있는 2행정 저속엔진 대비 좋은 수익성을 보여줄 
것으로 기대되는 바, 데이터센터향 생산이 시작될 28년 하반기부터 점진적으로 엔진 
믹스 개선 및 마진 성장이 가속화될 것으로 전망한다.

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-10-16_Invest in Germany, Yes, But How Turning the investment gap into Europe’s biggest opportunity_00e2290c.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-10-16

Status: ok

### Page 1

1

In Summary  
• 
 The first 
 Invest in Germany  
Summit  
takes place in Berlin on 19 and 20 October 2026, 
asking why and how Germany can attract 
 investment. The “why”: a top
 -
tier location 
that chronically underinvests, including from abroad.  
 Germany is the world’s third
 -
largest economy (~EUR4.4trn GDP), the only G7 country with a AAA rating from all three 
major agencies, and the third most attractive investment destination in Europe in 
 a 
2026 
EY survey. It has 1,700 hidden champions in the Mittelstand  
and R&D at 3.1% of GDP. 
Foreign companies are already here  
with 
an  
inward FDI stock 
 exceed
 ing  
EUR
 1.
0
tn
. Yet 
between 2019 and 2025, total 
 gross fixed 
 investment in Germany fell 6.4%, while it rose 
6.7% in the rest of the eurozone and 20.2% in the U.S. The gap is around EUR390bn versus 
Europe and up to 
 EUR730bn versus the U.S. Foreign investors are not closing it: 
announced 
 greenfield 
 investment 
 projects fell 10% in 2025 to 548 and are 44% below 
2019, the lowest since 2009. German savers face the same problem. Households and 
corporations save more than they invest, 
 and the surplus goes abroad (net foreign assets 
of 87% of GDP) at returns about 5pp below the U.S. and close to 3pp below other 
European countries since 1975, even below domestic returns. If German savers earn more 
at home, so can international investors.  
• 
 The problem is not a lack of capital but a lack of sufficiently attractive opportunities 
to deploy it, for domestic and foreign investors alike.  
Net public investment averaged 
zero between 2002 and 2015. Years of underinvestment should have raised returns on 
new capital, but high energy and labor costs (compounded by the second
 -
highest OECD 
tax wedge), bureaucracy (up to EUR146bn of lost output pe
 r year, per ifo
 ) and skills 
shortages prevent it. Investors themselves point to Germany’s integration of industry, 
science and research, legal certainty and skilled workforce as strengths, and to energy 
costs as Europe’s main disadvantage as an investment location
 . 
Financing is also a weak 
point: corporate financing costs are higher than in France or Italy, partly due to a 
fragmented banking sector, and projects are often too small for institutional investors 
(11,000 local authorities, 800 municipal utilities).  
• 
 The equation is changing: the fiscal shift kick
 -
starts investment, and reforms and 
public risk
 -
sharing open the door to private capital, German and international.  
The 
debt
 -
brake reform lifts the deficit from 2
 -
3% to 4
-
5% of GDP until 2028, funding defense 
and infrastructure. The “Deutschlandfonds
 ” (EUR30bn of public guarantees and equity) 
aims to mobilize EUR130bn of private capital by covering risks that private investors will 
not take alone. Foreign capital is already responding: FDI inflows rose about 50% to 
EUR86bn in 2025
 . 
European sources 
 contributed more than 
 80% of the total and the UK 
was 
 the largest single investor at 31%. Domestic savings 
 could 
 add to the pool: shifting 
5% of household financial assets out of deposits would mobilize ~EUR450bn, repatriating 
10% of net foreign assets ~EUR400bn. The “Altersvorsorgedepot” (from 1 January 2027, 
~EUR150bn over five years) and the proposed “Kapitalrente
 ” (a public fund of 
~EUR560bn by 2040) would build a domestic institutional investor base, a natural co
 -
investor for international capital. But much of the Altersvorsorgedepot may flow into 
global funds unless Germany becomes more attractive.

Invest in Germany, Yes, But How?  
Turning the investment gap into Europe’s biggest opportunity

### Page 2

2

• 
 Germany’s investment gap can therefore become one of Europe’s largest investment 
opportunities, in four verticals: energy, digital infrastructure, transport and mobility, 
and defense.  
In energy, needs reach ~EUR1.3trn by 2045 (about 30% of 2025 GDP) for 
renewables, grids, storage and hydrogen. Projects are often too small for institutional 
investors (11,000 local authorities, 800 Stadtwerke), so the enablers are advisory 
support, stand
 ardized tenders and aggregation at district and county level, and low
 -
carbon FDI rose 25% across Europe in 2025. Digital infrastructure needs EUR275
 -
500bn 
by 2030 (6
 -
11% of GDP) for hardware, telecoms, software and data centers, and foreign 
capital is alre
 ady committing, for example Google, Microsoft and Nvidia. The constraint 
is grid connection and permitting, not capital, so connection approvals should be 
prioritized. Transport and mobility 
 has  
EUR166bn planned for 2025
 -
29 (EUR107bn rail, 
EUR52bn federal roads, EUR8bn waterways), with PPP 2.0 as the enabler: performance
 -
linked service
 -
level agreements and long
 -
term offtake contracts that tie returns to 
predictable cash flows. Defense financing i
 s ~EUR734bn over five years, enough to meet 
NATO’s 3.5% of GDP target for core defense by 2029, and defense FDI projects rose 84% 
across Europe in 2025, with simpler regulation and procurement as the enabler. Across 
all four, the common enablers are faster  
 permitting and procurement, grid access, 
standardized structures and performance
 -
based PPPs with long
 -
term offtake contracts. 
Success would lift potential growth and ease pressure on the social state. Failure would 
leave Germany with higher debt and littl
 e improvement in long
 -
term growth.

Germany’s chronic investment shortage despite appealing conditions  
Germany's recent economic weakness should not obscure its enduring strengths and in
 -
principle attractiveness 
for investment.  
After several years of stagnation, the country remains Europe's largest economy, one of the world's 
leading industrial and exporting nations and home to globally competitive companies, world
 -
class research 
institutions, deep pools of skilled labor and exc
 eptionally strong public institutions. For long
 -
term investors, 
Germany offers a rare combination of economic scale, technological capability, political stability and financial 
strength.  
• 
 Scale matters: Germany is the EU's largest and the world's third
 -
largest economy (~EUR4.4trn GDP) 
meaning every investment project offers access to a substantial domestic market.  
Germany is also the 
third largest exporter, with one of the world's deepest manufacturing ecosystems, strong in machinery, 
chemicals, electrical equipment, vehicles pharmaceuticals, industrial automation and precision 
engineering, carried by a broad netwo
 rk of medium
 -
sized specialist firms. The 2026 EY attractiveness 
survey ranks Germany as the third most attractive investment destination in Europe, with a narrowing gap 
towards the top spots (France and UK).  
• 
 Its AAA rating and top institutional quality provide a stable framework for investors.  
Germany is the only 
G7 country that enjoys a AAA rating from the top three agencies, indicative of comfortably solid public 
finances. It has the top Rule of Law score in the G20 and ranks among the top 20 out of 208 countries in the 
World Bank’s various G
 overnance Indicators, reflecting strong rule of law, effective institutions, democratic 
accountability and relatively low corruption. This institutional strength provides a predictable and stable 
framework for businesses.  
• 
 The “Mittelstand
 :” 1700 hidden champions beyond the 40 global DAX companies form a uniquely strong 
economic ecosystem.  
Around 25% of German exports originate from medium
 -
sized companies that are 
niche B2B world market leaders. These hidden champions are strong innovators, filing patents at ~5x the 
rate of large corporates per employee. Many are privately held and often fa
 mily
-
owned.  
• 
 Germany is an innovation powerhouse and automation leader.  
Ranked 11th out of 139 economies in the 
2025 WIPO Global Innovation Index, it hosts seven of the world's leading innovation clusters and performs 
strongly in science, patent generation and logistics, where it ranks third globally. It is the world's fourth  
most 
automated manufacturing economy, with 449 industrial robots per 10,000 manufacturing workers (vs. a 
global average of 132). Its commitment to innovation is reflected in R&D spending equivalent to 3.1% of 
GDP, placing it 10th among roughly 80 countrie
 s tracked and ahead of the OECD average of 2.7%. Foreign

### Page 3

3

investors see this similarly: they name the close integration of industry, science and research, legal certainty 
and the skilled workforce as positives of Germany.
 1 
• 
 Human capital and scientific excellence.  
German nationals have won 17 Nobel prizes in scientific fields in 
the last 30 years alone, most recently this year (for medicine on 5 October). Max Planck Society researchers 
have been involved in 31 Nobel prizes since the institution’s founding
 . Three German universities are listed 
in the global top 50 on two different major international rankings: the Technical University of Munich, LMU 
Munich, which scored a perfect 100% on industry connections, and the University of Heidelberg. The country 
al
 so has the highest share of STEM graduates in the OECD.  
• 
 Quality of life and attractiveness for global talent.  
Three German cities make the global top 10 in Mercer’s 
most recent Quality of Living ranking (Frankfurt, Munich and Duesseldorf). Moreover, Germany is not only 
among the frontrunners in tackling climate change but also ranks sixth in Yale’s Environmental 
Performance Index, reinforcing its appeal as a destination for international talent.  
Despite these structural advantages, a massive investment gap has opened. 
 A key macroeconomic fact about 
Germany is its chronic lack of investment, which holds back capital accumulation, growth, and productivity. 
Compared to the U.S., the investment gap is very large (Figure 1): while U.S. fixed investment has increased by a 
factor of 2  
compared to the beginning of the millennium, Germany’s has increased only by 
 20%
. Even compared to 
its EU peers such as France, Sweden, or even the eurozone average, however, German investment lags.  
What’s more, German investment has declined outright since the pandemic. 
 Between 2019 and 2025, aggregate 
investments in Germany declined by 
 -
6.4%, compared with +6.7% in the rest of the Eurozone and +20.2% in the U.S. 
which alone leaves an investment gap of around EUR390bn versus Europe or EUR730bn vs the U.S. Foreign 
investor
s are not closing this gap. 
 T
he number of foreign investment projects announced in Germany fell by 10% in 
2025 to 548, the lowest level since 2009.
 2 
The decline was smaller than in France (
 -
17%) and the UK (
 -
14%
) but  
compared with 2019 Germany has lost more: 
 -
44%, against 
 -
28% in France and 
 -
34% in the UK (Figure 2). Unlike in 
France and the UK, the number has fallen every year since 2019.  
Foreign companies are nevertheless well represented in Germany. The stock of inward FDI stood at 
 EUR
 1.
0
tn at the 
end of 2024.
 3 
The German investment promotion agency GTAI recorded 1,564 foreign investment projects in 2025, 
9.3% fewer than in 2024 but a smaller decline than in the EU (
 -
18.1%) and worldwide (
 -
9.5%).

The composition of investment is skewed away from productivity
 -
enhancing items.  
Germany is a laggard in capex 
related to information and communication technology (ICT 
 – 
Figure 3), with ICT
 -
related investment less than half 
the OECD average as a share of GDP. These are investment items that are crucial determinants of productivity 
grow
 th.

External shocks don’t explain investment underperformance. 
 External shocks 
 – 
 such as the 2007
 -
08 financial 
crisis, Covid
 -
19, Russia’s full
 -
scale invasion of Ukraine, or the Iran war 
 – 
no doubt played a role. Energy price shocks 
have suppressed investment in Germany’s energy
 -
intensive industrial sectors. However, g
 iven that Germany’s 
investment underperformance is chronic, and that its peers were subject to the same shocks, these shocks only offer 
a partial explanation for Germany’s lack of investment.

### Page 5

5

Figure 4: Gross investment versus gross saving in % of GDP (2024): Germany saves but does not invest.

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Bird’s eye view: plenty of saving, not enough investment. 
 Germany’s lack of investment is particularly noteworthy 
given the fact that the economy generates large saving flows (Figure 4). National income accounting identities tell 
us that the gap between saving (S) and investment (I) is an economy’s current accoun
 t (CA) balance: CA = S 
 – 
I or, 
when abstracting from income flows on foreign assets, its export surplus.
 5  
Because the identity holds for the economy as a whole and for each sector, we can trace the surplus to its origins by 
looking at the saving
 -
investment balance of households, government, and financial and non
 -
financial corporations 
(NFC), which together add  
up to the current account (Figure 5). It turns out that, in addition to households’ persistent 
surplus of saving over investment, NFC have also been exhibiting saving surpluses. The government now invests 
more than it saves, after having made positive con
 tributions to the CA balance over much of the 2010s. In turn, CA 
surpluses imply capital exports: the excess of saving over investment is simply invested abroad.  
Figure 5: Saving net of investment by sector, and current account balance (% of GDP): Households have not been 
the only savers

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

5 
Ignoring primary income on net foreign assets and international transfers, the national income identity can be written as Y =  
C + G + I + NX, 
that is, GDP equals the sum of consumption, government spending, investment, and net exports. This can be rearran
 ged to give net exports as 
the difference between saving and investment NX = Y 
 – 
(C + G) 
 – 
I = S 
– 
I since national saving is the excess of GDP over domestic demand from 
consumption and government spending S = Y 
 – 
(C + G). In fact, Germany’s primary income  
balance (income earned on net foreign assets) is 
now almost as large as net exports
 . 
See Bundesbank (2026), Germany’s balance of payments in 2025, Monthly Report, March 2026.

### Page 6

6

Large foreign assets, poor returns  
Germany’s net foreign assets are 87% of GDP
 . Germany has amassed a large amount of claims against the rest of 
the world: its net international investment position (NIIP) 
 – 
 the difference between foreign assets and foreign 
liabilities 
 – 
is about 87% of GDP (Figure 6). From a macroeconomic perspectiv
 e, capital exports can in principle be 
beneficial for a country if it earns higher returns abroad than at home: the proof of the pudding is in the eating.  
Figure 6: Germany’s Net International Investment Position (USD bn): Savings go abroad.

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Returns on foreign assets: low 
 – 
and inferior to domestic returns
 . Germany has earned a poor return on its foreign 
assets for decades. Since 1975, its average annual foreign returns were about 5 percentage points below those of 
the U.S. and close to 3 points below those of other European countries (Table 1). What’s more
 , Germany’s foreign 
asset returns were considerably lower than the returns on its domestic assets: domestic saving has been 
systematically directed to foreign assets that earn less than the investment opportunities available at home.
 6 
Table 1: Returns on foreign assets 1975-2020 by country: Germany showing subdued returns on its foreign 
investments.

Source: Hünnekes et al. (2025). 
This suggests that Germany’s current account surplus 
 – 
or its export surplus 
 – 
turns out not to be a “good deal.” 
Instead, more investment at home would be optimal from a macroeconomic perspective. But it also begs the 
question: what are the reasons for Ge
 rmany’s excess saving?

6

### Page 7

7

The 
 multiple 
 origins of chronic investment shortage  
Households are 
 risk
 -
averse.  
 Germany’s high level of saving is usually attributed to prudent, highly risk
 -
averse 
households. Indeed, German households’ saving rate out of disposable income (Figure 7) is high in international 
comparison. At the same time, it is “natural” for the house
 hold sector in aggregate to feature a surplus of saving 
over investment. Households face an uncertain income stream but wish to smooth consumption. Consequently, they 
spread resources over a lifetime through saving  
making household surpluses a common feature of most economies.

Figure 7: Household gross saving in % of disposable income (2025): German households are heavy savers.

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Together with other factors such as risk aversion, the lack of a “Kapitalmarktkultur” (“capital markets culture”) and 
financial literacy
 7
, the institutional setup of pensions provision affects household saving incentives substantially: The 
public pay
 -
as
 -
you
-
go pension system reduces the need for households to bear market risk for retirement provision. 
As a result, German households hold 37%  
of their financial assets in bank deposits (Figure 8).  
Figure 8: Asset classes as % of total gross financial assets: Bank deposits still a key saving vehicle

Figure 5 suggests that, in addition to households, two more sectors have in recent years tended to have a surplus of

The lack of government investment contributed to a lack of demand and poor infrastructure. 
 Government 
surpluses of saving over investment partly reflected a lack of public investment. Between 2002 and 2015 net 
government investment (gross investment less depreciation on the public capital stock) was zero, on average 
(Figure 9). The government ba
 rely invested enough to keep the public capital stock from shrinking.  
Only since 2015 gross investment has been sufficient to keep net investment positive. At 3.5% of GDP in 2025, 
government gross investment  
is now catching up with peers. As a result of this lack of government investment, 
Germany’s infrastructure has deteriorated, which is likely to have contributed to the lack of domestic investment by 
firms.

Corporate surpluses persist.  
Figure 5 shows that the non
 -
financial corporate (NFC) sector has exhibited a surplus 
of saving over investment since around the turn of the millennium. Corporate saving is  
simply retained earnings, i.e. 
profits kept in the firm after paying interest, taxes and dividends. These surpluses became substantial after the great 
financial crisis and have persisted since at macro
 -
significant levels (on average since 2010: 1.6 % of G
 DP; 2025 still 
1.1% of GDP). While an excess of saving over investment is “natural” for households, it is not necessarily the case for 
NFCs. Firms invest to earn  
returns and will usually borrow to invest (or issue equity). Figure 10 shows the persistent 
shortfall of NFC investment vs saving.  
Figure 10: German non-financial corporations, gross saving vs gross investment, % of GDP: The gap persists

There are several reasons for the NFC surplus.  
• 
 Wage moderation and strong external demand
 . Real wages in the NFC sector have lagged productivity 
for nearly two decades (Figure 11). Only recently has real compensation for employees caught up. All else 
equal, wages lagging productivity would have boosted firms’ profits. Strong external demand, i
 f not 
accompanied by a commensurate increase in investment spending, will do the same. As shown in Figure 
1
0
, gross NFC saving jumped during periods of strong global demand (2009
 -
11 and after the pandemic, 
2020
 -
22). 
• 
 Lack of domestic demand
 . Wage moderation and a lack of public investment contributed to sluggish 
domestic demand, in turn suppressing economic growth. One consequence was that it made investment in 
faster
 -
growing overseas markets more attractive.  
• 
 Tax incentives favoring retained earnings.  
German corporate tax reforms (2001, 2008) made retaining 
profits more attractive than paying dividends, lowering payout rates and raising NFC gross saving.
 9 
These 
incentives matter in Germany because a large share of corporate profits are generated by privately held 
firms. In privately held firms, owners’ personal tax incentives, rather than pure profit maximization, may 
shape payout decisions. As the additio
 nal saving was not absorbed by investment (Figure 10), it resulted in 
higher net lending.  
Figure 11: Real compensation vs real value added per hour in the non -financial corporate sector (index, 2000 = 
100): wage restraint has contributed to non-financial sector surpluses until recently

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Economic theory suggests that years of underinvestment should raise expected returns and attract capital. In 
Germany, however, a range of supply
 -
side constraints is preventing this adjustment from taking place. 
 Energy 
costs remain elevated following the loss of cheap Russian pipeline gas and the shift towards more expensive LNG 
imports, compounded by high taxes and network charges. Businesses surveyed by EY in 2026 also see the cost of 
energy as Europe’s main dis
 advantage as an investment location, although 60% of them expect Europe’s 
attractiveness to improve over the next three years.
 10 
Labor costs are also burdened by Germany's exceptionally 
high tax wedge, the second highest in the OECD. Bureaucracy further weighs on competitiveness. According to the 
ifo economic research institute, excessive administrative requirements reduce annual o
 utput by up to EUR 146bn, 
or roughly 4% of GDP.  
11 
Although Germany ranks 12th out of 45 countries for overall business attractiveness, it ranks 
second
 -
to
-
last on costs.  
12 
Other structural weaknesses are equally apparent. Demographic decline is reducing the 
supply of skilled labor, while transport, logistics and digital infrastructure bear the consequences of years of 
underinvestment. Financing conditions are less favorable  
than in France or Italy, despite lower German risk
 -
free 
rates, with the fragmented banking system likely playing a role. These long
 -
standing challenges are now being 
compounded by structural shocks. Competition from China has intensified to the point that  
Germany runs a negative 
trade balance with China even in capital goods, for decades its core strength. Meanwhile, parts of the automotive 
sector misjudged both technology and market positioning, favoring combustion engines and fuel cell technology

What needs to be done: Germany’s fiscal push is a first step, but private capital needs to 
follow  
Germany’s fiscal push heralds potential investment turnaround. 
 Yet at least some of the demand
 -
side reasons for 
Germany’s large surpluses of saving over investment are on the mend. The German government has put in place a 
large fiscal expansion centered on infrastructure capex and defense. 
 Following the reform of its debt brake in 2025, 
public deficits are expected to rise from around 2
 -
3% to 4
-
5% of GDP in the next years, funding hundreds of billions 
of euros in additional spending on infrastructure, defense, energy, climate mitigation & ad
 aption and digitalization
 . 
The government’s fiscal package thus promises to kill two birds with one stone when it comes to improving German 
investment perspectives. In the short run it boosts investment spending, while in the longer run it improves public 
infrastructure, spurring 
 private investment demand.  
Government money alone is not enough: attracting private capital is necessary.  
Closing Germany’s investment 
gap will require more than public funding. The scale of the country’s investment needs is such that substantial 
private capital must also be mobilized. Foreign capital is already responding to some extent. 
 B
ased on Bundesbank 
data, FDI inflows rose by about 50% to around EUR86bn in 2025, after a fall of almost 32% in 2024  
(Figure 1
 2
)
.
13 
Investors from the UK raised their investment by 284% to EUR26bn and are now the largest single source (31% of 
the total), while U.S. investment fell by 44% to EUR11.8bn. Other EU countries contributed EUR43bn, so European 
investors account for more than 
 80% of the total.  
Figure 12: Foreign direct investment inflows to Germany, EUR bn and % of GDP: Rebound in the making

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Germany's challenge is not a shortage of capital but rather directing this capital towards productive investment.  
German households hold around EUR9trn in financial assets, a large proportion of which, by international 
standards, sits in cash and bank deposits (EUR3.4trn).
 14
. Meanwhile, the country's net foreign asset position exceeds 
EUR4.0trn (87% of GDP).  Unlocking even a small portion of these savings could have a transformative impact. 
Shifting just 5% of German households' financial assets out of deposits into more pro
 ductive assets would mobilize 
roughly EUR450bn of investment capital. In addition, repatriating for example 10% of the country’s net foreign 
investments would translate to EUR400bn of additional domestic capital.  
Making Germany more attractive for private investment starts with raising expected returns.  
 Years of 
underinvestment have already increased the potential returns on capital, as scarcity raises the marginal product of 
capital. But unlocking this opportunity requires reducing regulatory barriers, expanding standardized investment 
structures and st
 rengthening the investment culture through tax incentives, pension and insurance reforms, public
 -
private partnerships standards and strategic investment funds. Bottlenecks such as permitting, procurement, deal 
structuring and regulatory friction must be re
 duced. A key enabler for all relevant investment opportunities is

“Water, water, everywhere, nor any drop to drink”: Germany’s intermediation problem.  
The issue of available 
saving versus domestic investment 
 – 
or the country’s current account surplus 
 – 
is in large part about 
 where  
Germany 
invests. A related, but separate, question is how the savings that stay domestic are used. It turns out that another 
factor holding back investment in Germany is the intermediation of existing savings. Germany, like many other EU 
countries, is par

Key reform: towards a more funded retirement system
 . Scandinavia and the Netherlands, economies with similar 
“culture” to Germany, have much higher stock market capitalization as a share of GDP not least because households 
accumulate claims on private pensions, which in turn recycle savings into the domest
 ic equity market (Figure 1
 4
).
16 
The development of pension funds is the key reform because they are upstream of venture capital and private 
equity in the financing chain.

Sources: Eurostat, national central banks, financial supervisory authorities, financial associations and statistical

The pension commission’s proposal for a funded “Kapitalrente” financed by an additional 2% contribution

 .

 -

 -

 -

-

household retirement saving into equity markets at scale. According to estimates, the public fund could reach 
around EUR560bn by 2040 (8% of GDP) and become one of  
the world’s largest pension funds within two decades.
 18 
This would create a domestic institutional investor base that sits upstream from venture capital and private equity.  
As an additional pillar 3 contribution to Germany's pension system, the forthcoming “Altersvorsorgedepot” can 
also strengthen domestic investment.  
This reform, which has already been passed, will replace the Riester pension 
from 1 January 2027. Savers will be able to invest in shares, funds and ETFs without a capital guarantee
 . 
The state 
will pay up to 50 cents for every euro saved, up to a total of 360 euros, and an additional 25 cents for every euro 
saved up to a total of 1,800 euros. This results in a maximum annual subsidy of 540 euros.

How to invest in Germany: focus on key investment themes and incentives  
From underinvestment to investment opportunity 
 – 
why now is the right time to close Germany’s up to EUR730bn 
investment gap.  
Germany's investment gap presents both a problem and an opportunity. Years of underinvestment 
have created enormous modernization needs and, in principle, high marginal returns to new capital. Until now, 
structural bottlenecks prevented investors from cap
 turing those returns. The combination of fiscal expansion, reform 
and public
 -
private risk sharing could now begin to change that equation.  The key question that arises next is where 
and how to invest. The answer can be found in four key areas: energy, dig
 ital infrastructure, transport and mobility 
and defense.  
Energy transition:  
The move towards net
 -
zero emissions and greater geopolitical resilience will require substantial 
investment in renewable energy, electricity grids, storage solutions and hydrogen infrastructure. Germany will 
require around EUR1.3tn in investments for rene
 wables, grid infrastructure, storage and hydrogen by 2045, which 
is equivalent to 30% of GDP in 2025.  Financing vehicles and project pipelines are key enablers. However, with 
around 11,000 local authorities and 800 municipal utilities (“Stadtwerke”), indi
 vidual projects are often too small to 
attract institutional investors. To address this, municipalities and regions should be supported through advisory 
services, standardized tender frameworks and training programs to facilitate the structuring of investa
 ble public
 -
private partnerships. Greater participation by districts (“
 Regierungsbezirke”) and counties (“Kreise”) would facilitate 
project aggregation and create scalable investment pipelines. Foreign investors are showing interest here: 
according to EY, the number of low
 -
carbon energy FDI projects in Europe rose by 25% in 2
 025.
21 
Digital infrastructure:  
 Digitalization and digital infrastructure are critical drivers of productivity growth. It is 
estimated that a cumulative investment of between EUR275bn and 500bn will be required by 2030, equivalent to 
6
–
11% of GDP in 2025. This includes spending on compu
 ter hardware, telecommunications equipment, software 
and AI
 -
related infrastructure, such as data centers. Grid access and permitting reform are key enablers. Germany 
should therefore prioritize grid
 -
connection approvals and permitting procedures for data c
 enters and digital 
infrastructure, treating computing capacity with the same urgency as energy security. Multi
 -
year connection queues 
have become the primary constraint on Germany’s AI and digital expansion, rather than a lack of capital or demand. 
Foreign  
capital is already 
 committing: Google plans to invest EUR5.5bn in Germany until 2029, Microsoft announced

19 
Indefi (2026): The German Pension Reform and the Shape of Things to Come

In addition, the “Frühstartrente” would extend capital
 -
funded provision to children: the state pays 
 EUR
 10 monthly into a depot from age six 
until 18.

 g is 
set to vote on this measure on 16 October 2026.

EUR3.2bn in 2024 and Nvidia is part of a EUR1bn data center project with Deutsche Telekom in Munich.

declined.  
Transport and mobility: 
 Modernizing transport infrastructure is another major investment priority. The Ministry of 
Transport plans to invest EUR166bn between 2025 and 2029, including EUR107bn for rail, EUR52bn for federal 
roads, and EUR8bn for waterways. A key enabler is performa
 nce
-
based concessions. A new generation of public
 -
private partnerships (PPP 2.0) should be based on performance
 -
linked service
 -
level agreements and long
 -
term 
offtake contracts that link investor returns to measurable and predictable cash flows.  
Defense: 
 German defense financing is expected to reach approximately EUR734bn over five years, enabling the 
country to meet NATO's target of spending 3.5% of GDP on core defense by 2029. Planned procurement includes 
land systems, air power, air and missile defense,  
long
 -
range strike capabilities, unmanned systems, munitions, and 
space security assets. Foreign investors are also moving in: the number of defense FDI projects in Europe rose by 
84% in 2025 to 107.
 23 
Only seven of them were in Germany, against 20 in the UK, 14 in France, 12 in Ukraine, 10 in 
Spain and nine in Poland (Figure 14).  Key enabler: simplification. As in other sectors, reducing regulatory and 
procurement complexity will be crucial to acceler
 ating investment and delivery.

What is at stake  
Germany has a rare opportunity to transform today's fiscal stimulus into a sustained investment boom. 
 If 
successful, the country could benefit significantly from a renewed capital influx. Not only would it lift growth from 
the demand side in the short run, it would also raise potential growth and boost long
 -
term growth for decades. The 
benefits would exten
 d beyond Germany 
 – 
Europe cannot afford for its largest economy to remain its weakest link.  
The gains would be economic and political. 
 Stronger growth, functioning infrastructure and rising prosperity would 
help to restore confidence in institutions, strengthening the foundations of the social state and reducing some of the 
economic drivers behind the current political polarization. Howev
 er, successful and bold reforms, as well as 
investors willing to support Germany's renewed momentum, are equally essential to achieving this outcome.  
Failure would come at a high cost. 
 While fiscal spending could provide a temporary boost, structural weaknesses 
would persist, leaving Germany with higher debt and little improvement in long
 -
term growth. The consequences 
would extend beyond the economic sphere, including continued dependenc
 e on foreign energy and technology, 
further erosion of Europe's stability and competitiveness, and continued political polarization and populism. 
Germany's investment challenge is therefore about much more than just growth. It concerns the future economic 
and political resilience of Germany and Europe as a whole.

Document 168: Anthropic被曝秘密游说国会：AI造成超10亿美元损失，政府才能叫停？

Source PDF: all report pdf/Anthropic被曝秘密游说国会：AI造成超10亿美元损失，政府才能叫停？.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-17
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

据 Politico 报道， Anthropic 正秘密向国会游说⼀项前沿 AI 安全监管法案草案。草案规定，
仅当模型存在造成逾 10 亿美元损失或超 50 ⼈伤亡的预测性即时⻛险时，政府⽅可⼲预。草
案要求开发者开展独⽴评估与透明化测试，并由指定法院最终裁决是否暂停模型。
摘要：
Anthropic 正悄然推动⼀项由其主导起草的 AI 安全法案，试图为联邦政府⼲预前沿 AI 模型设定极
⾼⻔槛，同时将监管权⼒严格限定于商务部和联邦法院。
据路透社 17 ⽇援引 Politico 报道， Anthropic 代表已私下向国会议员游说这份草案法规。根据法案
内容，政府只有在 AI 模型⾯临可预⻅的、迫在眉睫的⻛险 —— 即可能造成超过 10 亿美元损失，
或导致 50 ⼈以上死亡或重伤时，⽅可介⼊阻⽌模型发布或使⽤。这⼀⾼⻔槛意味着，绝⼤多数
情形下 AI 模型的开发与部署将不受政府⼲预。
Anthropic 发⾔⼈向路透社表⽰，该法案所体现的提案内容，源⾃ Anthropic 今年早些时候发布

的 " 先进 AI 框架 " （ Advanced AI Framework ）。发⾔⼈同时强调，法案将赋予政府 " 阻⽌或遏制最
强⼤模型危险部署的能⼒ " ，并要求开发者对前沿 AI 模型进⾏测试、公开评估结果，并接受独⽴
第三⽅评估。商务部未就路透社的置评请求作出回应。
触发监管的条件极为严苛
根据 Politico 的报道，法案对政府介⼊条件作出严格限定。触发监管的⻛险必须源于以下三种特
定情形之⼀： AI 模型被⽤于⽣物武器的研制或扩散、模型逃脱开发者的控制，或模型引发⽹络
攻击。
这意味着，仅仅是模型可能带来的⼴泛社会影响或经济竞争扭曲，并不构成政府叫停的法定依
据。批评者可能认为，上述条件设置过于狭窄，实际上为 Anthropic 等前沿 AI 实验室的持续运营
提供了相当程度的法律屏障。
独⽴评估机构居核⼼地位，但由实验室付费
法案设计了⼀套以独⽴评估机构为核⼼的监督机制。这些评估机构须在商务部登记注册，并负
责定期对实验室及其前沿模型进⾏评估。
值得关注的是，法案允许 AI 实验室向这些评估机构⽀付费⽤，但明确规定报酬不得与评估结论挂

钩。⼀旦独⽴评估机构判定某⼀前沿模型存在 " 迫在眉睫的列举⻛险 " ，须在完成评估后 72 ⼩时内
向商务部报告。
这⼀付费安排或引发外界对评估独⽴性的质疑，尽管法案试图以 " 报酬与结论脱钩 " 的条款加以防
范。
监管权⼒⾼度集中，其他机构明确排除在外
在权⼒边界上，法案作出了明确且严格的划定。商务部可在认定⻛险后向法院申请暂停模型运
营，由哥伦⽐亚特区联邦地区法院的联邦法官作出最终裁决。
然⽽，法案同时明⽂禁⽌美国其他任何政府部⻔、机构或官员暂停 AI 模型的开发、部署或内部使
⽤。这⼀条款将监管权⼒⾼度集中于商务部和特定联邦法院，有效阻⽌了其他联邦机构 —— 包
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

Zooming out, 10yr French OATs have seen their worst decade of nominal returns since 1803,
when the Reign of Terror (1793-94) was still part of the 10yr rolling calculations.

Recent concerns have pushed France’s 10yr spread over Germany’s, to its highest since 2012
and the Euro crisis. But in an historical perspective, today’s spreads are only high by the
standards of the Euro Area when the two shared a common currency. They were regularly
several percentage points apart in the 70s and 80s.
Franco-German 10yr yield spread (percentage points)
Source: Finaeon, Deutsche Bank

Jim Reid | France in a Long-term Perspective… | October 2026

Although much of the recent selloff is an unwinding of carry trades, France has ongoing, if not
new, fundamental issues. It hasn’t run a budget surplus since 1974, and the deficit was last
beneath 2% of GDP in 2001.
French Government Budget Deficit (% of GDP) over the last 200 years

Source: Finaeon, Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank
Jim Reid | France in a Long-term Perspective… | October 2026

But with half a century of persistent deficits, France’s debt-to-GDP ratio has seen a near-
continuous rise – to 116% in 2025 under the Maastricht definition.
French Debt (% of GDP)

France has also seen its debt rise in relative terms. As recently as the GFC in 2008, France’s
debt-to-GDP was basically in line with Germany’s and well below Italy’s, but today it’s far
above Germany’s and closing in on Italy’s. Outside of the temporary Covid spike, Italian
debt/GDP has been stable for over a decade.
Gross Public Debt-to-GDP Ratios

Jim Reid | France in a Long-term Perspective… | October 2026
Source: IMF , Deutsche Bank

For most of the last decade, nominal GDP has been above 10yr yields, but that has reversed,
which is challenging from a debt sustainability if sustained – especially when debt/GDP is
pushing up against 120%. Recent inflation will slowly push up nominal GDP but the
relationship between the two will be important going forward.

French nominal GDP growth (year-on-year % change) and 10yr yields
Source: Finaeon, Deutsche Bank
Jim Reid | France in a Long-term Perspective… | October 2026

Inflation is always a double-edged sword when it comes to debt. An unexpected burst of
inflation can help lower debt, as we saw right after the pandemic, but it becomes a challenge
when higher yields offset this. This is now the 3rd biggest inflation spike since the Euro Area’s
formation in 1999.
French HICP Inflation (year-on-year % change)
Source: Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank

Even in the period when France has run 50-plus years of consecutive deficits, its average
inflation has been broadly around US levels (c.4%) and lower than the likes of the UK, Italy and
Spain.

economy has averaged less than 2% inflation, but Switzerland at 2.2% comes closest

The recent underperformance is clear for equities too – and has been for over two years.
France’s CAC 40 has recently underperformed all the major European equity indices: the
Europe-wide STOXX 600 is up over 30% in that time, and the DAX is up over 50%, yet the CAC
40 is up only 4%.

All that said, DB’s rates strategists now see French bonds as cheap relative to their
fundamental metrics. This chart looks at the residuals of France’s twin deficit score, which is a
weighted average of the historical current account balance (% of GDP) and budget balance (%
of GDP), with a higher weight assigned to more recent years.
France residual vs Twin Deficit model

Source: Bloomberg Finance LP , Deutsche Bank
Jim Reid | France in a Long-term Perspective… | October 2026

Looking at a variety of macro variables, they now see France as the cheapest European
government bond relative to fundamentals. So things are challenging at the moment, and the
long-term charts look worrying, but much of the sell-off in recent days/weeks has been due to a
clearing-out of carry trades, implemented assuming a much lower vol period than we’ve seen.

EGB 10y yields' residuals vs various Macro variables
Source: IMF , Bloomberg Finance LP , Deutsche Bank

Document 261: Lipper - This Week in Earnings 26Q3

Source PDF: all report pdf/Lipper - This Week in Earnings 26Q3.pdf
Report source: Refinitiv Lipper

### Page 1

PROPRIETARY RESEARCH FROM LSEG 
THIS WEEK IN EARNINGS

This Week in Earnings provides analysis and commentary on aggregate  
earnings estimate revisions, growth rates and valuations.

ANALYST: Tajinder Dhillon, CFA October 1, 2026 
tajinder.dhillon@lseg.com; 
THIS WEEK IN EARNINGS 
AGGREGATE ESTIMATES AND REVISIONS 
 26Q3 Y/Y earnings are expected to be 30.6%. Excluding the energy sector, the Y/Y earnings estimate is 26.8%. 
 Of the 13 companies in the S&P 500 that have reported earnings to date for 26Q3, 84.6% have reported earnings 
above analyst estimates. This compares to a long-term average of 67.7% and prior four quarter average of 81.5%. 
 26Q3 Y/Y revenue is expected to be 11.6%. Excluding the energy sector, the growth estimate is 11.2%. 
 76.9% of companies have reported 26Q3 revenue above analyst expectations. This compares to a long-term 
average of 63% and an average over the past four quarters of 76.8%. 
 For 26Q3, there have been 40 negative EPS preannouncements issued by S&P 500 corporations compared to 65 
positive EPS preannouncements. By dividing 40 by 65 the N/P ratio is 0.6 for the S&P 500 Index.  
 The forward four-quarter (26Q4– 27Q3) P/E ratio for the S&P 500 is 20.1. 
 During the week of Oct. 5, three S&P 500 companies are expected to report quarterly earnings. 
SECTION A: EARNINGS OUTLOOK 
Q3 2026: EARNINGS REVISIONS 
EXHIBIT 1A.  S&P 500: Q3 2026 SHARE-WEIGHTED EARNINGS ($B)

Source: LSEG I/B/E/S

### Page 2

PROPRIETARY RESEARCH FROM LSEG OCTOBER 1, 2026 
THIS WEEK IN EARNINGS 
This Week in Earnings provides analysis and commentary on aggregate earnings estimate revisions, growth rates and valuations.  2 
There has been an increase in the share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 since the start of the quarter (to $773.2B 
from $760.7B). Seven of the eleven sectors have experienced downward revisions to estimates.

Since Aug. 1, the materials (-9.1%) and health care (-2.2%) sectors have recorded the highest percentage decreases 
in earnings, while the energy (16.3%) sector has recorded the highest percentage increase in earnings. Overall, share-
weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 1.6% since the start of the quarter.

Since Aug. 1, the health care (-1.7B) and materials (-1.5B) sectors have recorded the highest dollar-level decreases in 
earnings, while the information technology (8.3B) sector has recorded the highest dollar-level increase in earnings. 
Overall, expected share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 12.5B since the start of the quarter.

13 companies in the S&P 500 Index have reported earnings for Q3 2026. Of these companies, 84.6% reported 
earnings above analyst expectations and 7.7% reported earnings below analyst expectations. In a typical quarter 
(since 1994), 67% of companies beat estimates and 20% miss estimates. Over the past four quarters, 82% of 
companies beat the estimates and 15% missed estimates. 
In aggregate, companies are reporting earnings that are 5.3% above estimates, which compares to a long-term (since 
1994) average surprise factor of 4.4% and the average surprise factor over the prior four quarters of 7.7%.

13 companies in the S&P 500 Index have reported revenue for Q3 2026. Of these companies, 76.9% reported revenue 
above analyst expectations and 23.1% reported revenue below analyst expectations. In a typical quarter (since 2002), 
63% of companies beat estimates and 37% miss estimates. Over the past four quarters, 77% of companies beat the 
estimates and 23% missed estimates. 
In aggregate, companies are reporting revenues that are 2.4% above estimates, which compares to a long-term (since 
2002) average surprise factor of 1.3% and the average surprise factor over the prior four quarters of 2.5%.

The estimated earnings growth rate for the S&P 500 for 26Q3 is 30.6%. If the energy sector is excluded, the growth 
rate declines to 26.8%. The S&P 500 expects to see share-weighted earnings of $773.2B in 26Q3, compared to share-
weighted earnings of $591.9B (based on the year-ago earnings of the current 504 constituents) in 25Q3. 
11 of the 11 sectors in the index expect to see an improvement in earnings relative to 25Q3. The Energy and 
Information Technology sectors have the highest earnings growth rates for the quarter, while the Consumer Staples 
sector has the weakest anticipated growth compared to 25Q3. 
The Energy sector has the highest earnings growth rate (114.7%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $55.1B in 26Q3, 
compared to earnings of $25.7B in 25Q3. 4 of the 5 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see higher earnings 
than a year ago. The Oil & Gas Refining & Marketing (445.6%) and Integrated Oil & Gas (116.1%) sub-industries have 
the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate declines to 25.1%. 
The Information Technology sector has the second highest earnings growth rate (66.5%) of any sector. It is expected 
to earn $256.1B in 26Q3, compared to earnings of $153.8B in 25Q3. 12 of the 12 sub-industries in the sector are 
anticipated to see higher earnings than a year ago. The Semiconductors (135.6%) and Semiconductor Materials & 
Equipment (68.9%) sub-industries have the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, 
the growth rate declines to 25.3%. 
The Consumer Staples sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (3.2%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $33.7B 
in 26Q3, compared to earnings of $32.6B in 25Q3. 3 of the 10 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see lower 
earnings than a year ago. The Packaged Foods & Meats (-6.5%) and Household Products (-4.2%) sub-industries have 
the lowest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate improves to 6.4%.

The estimated earnings growth rate for the S&P 500 for 26Q4 is 28.5%. If the energy sector is excluded, the growth 
rate declines to 25.5%. The S&P 500 expects to see share-weighted earnings of $804.2B in 26Q4, compared to share-
weighted earnings of $625.7B (based on the year-ago earnings of the current 504 constituents) in 25Q4. 
11 of the 11 sectors in the index expect to see an improvement in earnings relative to 25Q4. The Energy and 
Information Technology sectors have the highest earnings growth rates for the quarter, while the Real Estate sector 
has the weakest anticipated growth compared to 25Q4. 
The Energy sector has the highest earnings growth rate (102.8%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $49.2B in 26Q4, 
compared to earnings of $24.3B in 25Q4. 3 of the 5 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see higher earnings 
than a year ago. The Oil & Gas Refining & Marketing (230.6%) and Integrated Oil & Gas (129.3%) sub-industries have 
the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate declines to 39.4%. 
The Information Technology sector has the second highest earnings growth rate (54.9%) of any sector. It is expected 
to earn $289.1B in 26Q4, compared to earnings of $186.6B in 25Q4. 12 of the 12 sub-industries in the sector are 
anticipated to see higher earnings than a year ago. The Semiconductors (117%) and Semiconductor Materials & 
Equipment (68.8%) sub-industries have the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, 
the growth rate declines to 18.4%. 
The Real Estate sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (4.2%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $15.2B in 
26Q4, compared to earnings of $14.6B in 25Q4. 2 of the 13 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see lower 
earnings than a year ago. The Telecom Tower Reits (-13.3%) and Multi-Family Residential Reits (-0.1%) sub-industries 
have the lowest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate improves to 7.1%.

The estimated revenue growth rate for the S&P 500 for 26Q3 is 11.6%. If the energy sector is excluded, the growth rate declines  to 11.2%. The S&P 
500 expects to see revenue of $4826.7B in 26Q3, compared to revenue of $4324.7B (based on the year-ago earnings of the current 504 
constituents) in 25Q3.

11 of the 11 sectors anticipate revenue growth for the quarter. The Information Technology sector has the highest revenue growth rate for the 
quarter, while the Health Care sector has the weakest anticipated growth compared to 25Q3.

The Information Technology sector has the highest revenue growth rate (39.5%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $764.6B in 26Q3, compared to 
revenue of $547.9B in 25Q3. 12 of the 12 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see higher revenue than a year ago. The Semiconductors 
(87.7%) and Semiconductor Materials & Equipment (48.2%) sub-industries have the highest revenue growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are 
removed, the growth rate declines to 22.1%.

The Health Care sector has the lowest revenue growth rate (4%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $958.4B in 26Q3, compared to revenue of 
$921.2B in 25Q3. 1 of the 10 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see lower revenue than a year ago. The Health Care Supplies (-3.1%) 
and Managed Health Care (1.2%) sub-industries have the lowest revenue growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed , the growth rate 
improves to 5.1%.

STARMINE EARNINGS SURPRISE FORECAST
Looking forward at quarterly performance, we use StarMine’s SmartEstimate® from LSEG to determine which
companies in the S&P 500 are better poised to beat and miss earnings estimates. The SmartEstimate® is a weighted

average of analyst estimates, with more weight given to more recent estimates and more accurate analysts. Our
studies have shown that when the SmartEstimate® differs significantly from the consensus (I/B/E/S mean estimate),
the Predicted Surprise accurately predicts the direction of earnings surprises or further revisions 70% of the time.
When a significant Predicted Surprise for revenue is also present for the period, the accuracy improves to 78%.

StarMine ARM from LSEG is an analyst revisions stock ranking model, designed to predict future changes in analyst
sentiment. ARM incorporates more accurate earnings estimates through the SmartEstimate prediction service. ARM
region rankings scores companies by region on a scale of 1 to 100 where 100 represents the most bullish sentiment.
Over the next two weeks, 31 S&P 500 companies are expected to report earnings. Of these companies, three positive

surprises and five negative surprises are expected from S&P 500 companies reporting quarterly results.

Report Introduction
Our proprietary dbDataInsights survey shows cyber threats are evolving faster than organisations can adapt
Source: dbDataInsights survey of cybersecurity experts, September 2026; Cybersecurity Survey 2026, Deutsche Bank Research.
“It’s the only thing that can shut down my business.”
That was the punchline from a conversation I had recently with the CEO of a large
privately held financial services company. This was his comment when I followed up

as to why cybersecurity risk is the number one concern that “keeps him up at night”.
To help understand this risk, our dbDataInsights team surveyed more than 50
cybersecurity experts. The uncomfortable conclusion is that many firms are
unprepared for cyber threats. Cyber risk is increasing as AI adoption accelerates.
—89% agree the threat landscape is evolving faster than organisations can adapt.
—78% say the gap between highly and poorly prepared organisations is widening.

What has changed with AI advancement? Cyberattacks have become faster,
cheaper, more deceptive and more scalable. Capabilities once reserved for
sophisticated actors are now broadly accessible to a new generation of profit-driven
criminals. At the same time, rising geopolitical tension has made cyber operations
an increasingly attractive low-cost, deniable tool for nation states. Corporates and
investors therefore need to understand their broader exposure and critical

infrastructure risk in their supply chain and with stakeholders.
Among other autonomous incidents, the recent Hugging Face incident
highlighted AI’s cyberattack potential. AI agents caused harm without
direction from a human attacker, with weak guardrails and no human
judgement to restrain them. Beyond the systemic questions it raised, the attack
highlights three additional concerns: (i) AI operates at a speed and scale humans
cannot match, with agents taking over 17,000+ actions in four days; (ii) the intrusion

began in the data-processing pipeline, where AI platforms are uniquely exposed;
and (iii) the agents escaped their test environment through a widely used third-party
tool representing supplier risk in an unfamiliar form.

### Page 4

Report summary and notable takeaways
Secular trends and AI adoption have widened the attack surface; the severity of losses is compounding
Source: FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3), company security advisories, OECD, company reports, Deutsche Bank Research.
Secular trends have widened the attack surface. This includes rapid
digitalisation, widespread Internet of Things (IoT) endpoints, hybrid workforces,
cloud adoption and deeper supply chain dependencies. The scale of the threat

increase is reflected in data from the FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3),
which shows cybercrime complaints more than doubled from 2019 pre-pandemic
baseline of ~467,000 to ~1.0mn in 2025, with a 3x expansion relative to 2017 levels.
Barriers to entry are lower; attack tools are now sold “off the shelf”. Today,
cybercrime marketplaces, available on the dark web, look less like the underworld
portrayed in films and more like an online software store. Buyers can access AI-

powered tools that scan for software vulnerabilities (see top-right chart showing
reported CVEs after the release of cyber capable frontier models), write malicious
code at speed, and provide access to malware that rewrites its own code to avoid
detection as it runs. Ransomware-as-a-service (RaaS) takes it further, renting out
ready-made attack kits, often in exchange for a portion of the profits.
Impersonation is becoming harder to detect. Hackers can now generate more

realistic phishing messages, while voice cloning and deepfakes make impersonation
convincing enough to gain access unnoticed. With stolen credentials, attackers often
aren’t hacking in, they are simply logging in.
The rise of loose cybercriminal collectives. Some of the most damaging recent
attacks have been linked to loosely organised, often English-speaking networks such
as ShinyHunters and Scattered Spider. The latter has been widely named as the

group behind the 2025 Marks & Spencer cyberattack, in which the hacking group
deployed RaaS. AI is enabling these groups to become larger and faster, widening
the gap between the speed of attack and a defender’s response.
The severity of losses is compounding. A 2026 IBM study puts the average breach
at a record USD ~5.0mn, up 12% YoY, but the impact on large companies can run
into hundreds of millions or even billions. Estimates for global cyber insurance

premiums (per Munich Re) point to a rise from roughly USD 7.0bn in 2020 to USD
15.0bn in 2025, with expectations to reach USD 28.0bn by 2030.

### Page 5

5
Among the more concerning survey takeaways is that a large-scale and
significant systemic incident is now the base case.
— 83% expect a significant systemic attack within two years and 81% expect it to be
moderately, very, or extremely severe for economic activity and markets.
— 81% say AI favours attackers today, with not one respondent putting defenders
ahead of attackers. Only 20% expect defenders to be ahead in five years.
Geopolitical tensions coupled with rising cyber capabilities have transformed

cyberspace into a digital battlefield between global powers. Intent has moved
towards espionage and disruption of not only critical infrastructure (e.g., healthcare,
finance, power, industrial systems, defence) but private companies as well. Chinese
President Xi Jinping has linked cybersecurity to national security and economic and
social stability, with China’s public statements similarly emphasising the protection
of critical infrastructure. At the same time, the US’s June 2025 Executive Order on

Cybersecurity reflects its continued focus on national cyber resilience.
Europe relies on US cyber tools while its start-ups lag in funding. Reliance on US
security vendors exposes European firms to potential access, update or export
restrictions over the long-term. Shrinking this gap will prove difficult given US cyber
start-ups attract nearly 6x the venture and growth funding of European peers. And
while the timing of quantum computing (which could break widely used encryption

methods) remains uncertain, it could further deepen Europe’s dependency.
Ultimately, cyber preparedness is a financial variable. Investors should ask what
firms spend on security versus AI adoption, how well they know their vulnerabilities,
and how fast they can recover.
— Only 24% of respondents think organisations have sufficient visibility into the
threats relevant to them, even though 57% saw cybersecurity budgets rise over
the past 12 months.

— 81% agree that cybersecurity related laws and regulation cannot keep pace, and
65% doubt leadership understands cyber risk well enough.

### Page 6

6
When cyber weakness = share price consequence…
Investors are more aware of cyber risk today, but they rarely sell a stock due to
perceived cyber vulnerabilities (although we are aware of some who have).
—What is the potential impact? Our dbDataInsights event study of 47 breaches
shows a cumulative breach-attributed return of c. -2.9% within the first two
trading days and c. -6.0% through first 20 days trading days. The erosion then
continues more gradually, reaching c. -6.7% nine months after disclosure (see

slide 13).
Which industries are most vulnerable?
—Our survey respondents point to (1) government & public sector (52%), (2)
financial services (50%), (3) energy and utilities (46%) and (4) healthcare and life
sciences follow (39%, rising to 48% among US respondents).
We should caveat, that high sector exposure does not mean every company in it is
equally vulnerable. Some firms in these “exposed” sectors may very well be better
prepared given they have built specialist teams and defence protocols for decades.

What about access to credit and ratings? Credit and rating providers are paying
attention. Moody’s and S&P both cut their outlook on Jaguar Land Rover within two
months of its 2025 hack. Today, this type of impact is not widespread. But if risk
escalates through a systemic incident or an AI-driven step change in attacks these
mechanisms, including ESG ratings, could reprice asset ownership or impact access
to credit. In fact, some lenders are already pricing it. Journal of Corporate Finance

research using US syndicated loan data found borrowers with persistently higher cyber
risk pay 4–13bp more.
The bottom line? While a broader systemic attack is hard to prepare for, corporates
and investors should be asking questions about vulnerabilities, security investment,
preparedness and recovery should an attack occur. This is especially true for
industries quickly adopting AI capabilities and / or a high threat landscape.
